Yes; I had seen him several times in the way of business - I saw no concealment about him - any one could see the wool in these bags, they were not finished sewing - I had not received any wool of Mr. Bell before - I had received strips of cloth, and gun cases.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Have you his residence in your book? A. Yes; "John Bell, of Old-street" - I asked him his address, and he gave me this, which is our general way.
JAMES SKILLMAN. I am a porter at Messrs. Pickford's wharf. I saw the prisoner there on Wednesday, the 12th of February, when these bags were brought on a truck - there was a hole in one of the bags, and I saw wool in it- the hole was sewed up, by Mr. Bell's desire - I saw him again the next day - he asked me if they could lay there three or four days or a week, or if there was a boat going, that we could send them off directly - he followed me down the stairs, and I asked the porter who was shipping at the scale, if he was shipping any thing for Leeds - he said,the boat for Leeds was not in, but he would call it in, and he did, and shipped the three bags in it - at Mr. Bell's request, I marked the bags Nos. 1, 2, and 3, and a D with L under it; and they were consigned to Mr. Dufton, of Leeds - they were shipped on board the Conqueror, Captain James Simpson - they appeared to me to be like one bag, cut into three parts, and they were packed clumsily.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did they appear to be like one rough bag cut into three smooth ones? A. No; they were different qualities of case - they appeared to be one cut into three - I did not see the consignment - Mr. Bell, said to me, if I would ship them directly, he would give me 1s.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You have said the bags had the appearance of different qualities cut out of one bag? A. It is different qualities, some of it is like sacking, and some like the regular bags.
Q. Will you tell us what you mean by one bag cut into three? A. It appears that the wool has been taken out and sewed up in separate bags - bags of wool are of a certain weight; but I do not weigh them.
WILLIAM BREED. I am porter at the shipping scale, at Pickford's wharf - I remember these three bags of wool being weighed to be shipped to Leeds - they weighed as follows: - No. 1 - 1 cwt. 2 qrs. 14 lbs. - No. 2 - 1 cwt. 3 qrs. 7 lbs. - No. 3 - 1 cwt. 2 qrs. 4 lbs. Total - 4 cwt. 3 qrs. 25 lbs. or 557 lbs. I have been in the habit of weighing bags of wool; they are all weights from 1 cwt. to 8 cwt.
JAMES SIMPSON. I am a boatman in Messrs. Pickford's employ - my boat is the Conqueror. On some day in February, I took these three bags of wool from their wharf to Leeds - I got as far as Huddersfield when it was stopped by the officer - it had not been out of my possession.
THOMAS RAYSON. I am clerk to Mr. Robert John Smith and Mr. Duff - they are the proprietors of Galley-quay, and take in goods as wharfingers - I remember eight bales of wool being taken in on the 11th of February, and on the next day, between three and four o'clock, I heard that one was missing - I examined the bales which were left, and one was missing - I do not know the number of it, but it had a triangle on it, with a circle at each corner - I went with Lee the officer to Mr. Bell's house, No. 16, Provost-street, City-road - that street does not lead directly into Old-street - we went about six o'clock in the evening of Thursday - the prisoner was not at home - we waited about a quarter of an hour - the prisoner came in, and either the officer or I asked him if he had had a bale of wool yesterday - he said he had not; and he asked Lee by what authority we were in his house; and if he had a warrant - he said he had been an officer himself, and his house ought not to be searched without some authority - Lee then showed his staff of office, and put several questions to him - I stated that I had lost a bale of wool - he asked if I was the prosecutor - I said I represented the prosecutors or the proprietors, I do not know which, but I think it was proprietors - the prisoner said he did not know it was stolen - I asked him from whom he bought it, but he did not make any answer to that - I asked him whether he paid for it by a cheque or by cash - he said he paid for it by a £50 note, and received a shilling back - he said he had no invoice, and had not entered it in his book - I asked him if he weighed it; and he said he had, and it weighed 549lbs. - I said, "You seem to have a specific weight: where did you weigh it? I do not see any scales"- he did not answer that question - I asked him where the bale of wool was - he did not answer - I then asked him if he had sold it - he said, "No" - I then said, "As you can give no satisfactory account from whom you bought it, or what you have done with it, it will be my duty to give you into custody" - he then said he would pay for the wool, and pay the officer his expenses, rather than be taken into custody - I said I could listen to no compromise; if he had bought it fairly, he could have no objection to state from whom he bought it - he then turned to Lee, and said,"You know him; I have not seen him before for six months" - we had left Wells outside, but after Mr. Bell had refused all knowledge of the bale of wool, we brought Wells in and confronted them - I said, "Do you know this gentleman, boy?" - Wells said, "Yes; that is the gentleman who helped me into the passage with the bale of wool yesterday" - the prisoner then said, "Yes, I did buy a bale of wool yesterday."
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. It was after you told him that the bale of wool had been stolen, that he said, whatever wool he had bought, he did not know had been stolen? A. Yes; I said we came to inquire after a bale of wool that had been stolen; but Lee said, "Have you bought a bag of German skin-wool?" - the subject of the mark was never mentioned - I should rather think that is not an ordinary kind of wool - there may be some in the market, but no great quantity, in proportion to the general quantity of wool; but I do not examine the wool - we do not hear of German skin-wool in nine cases out of ten - there may be thousands of pounds of it in the market - I know nothing about the price of German skin-wool; but Mr. Bell said, "I have given 1s. 10 1/2d. for this: I shall not get a half-penny a pound by it."
JAMES LEE. I am an officer. I was employed to go with Mr. Rayson to the prisoner - Wells went with us - Mr. Rayson went in first, and I went in afterwards - when we had waited about ten minutes, the prisoner came and knocked at the door - Mrs. Bell opened it, and said, "My dear, there are some officers here about some wool" - I was behind her - I asked him if his name was Bell - he said "Yes" - I said I wanted to speak to him - I then told him I called about a bale of wool that was brought to his house yesterday - he said, by what authority did I come: had I a warrant - I said "No;" but I told him my name, and what office I belonged to - I did not produce my staff - I then asked if he had received any wool - he said "No" - I asked him again; and he repeated two or three times that he had not - I told him to be particular - I then called Wells in, and said to him, "Do you know this person?" pointing to the prisoner - he said "Yes; that is the gentleman who assisted in taking in the bale of wool, and putting it down in the passage" - I said, "What do you say to that, Mr. Bell?" - he looked very earnestly at Wells, and then said, "Yes, I recollect that boy yesterday" - he then said that he had bought a bale of wool - Mr. Rayson then asked if he had an invoice of it - he said "No" - Iasked him repeatedly who he bought it of - he said he had not seen the person before for six months - I asked his name, and where he lived; but he would not give any answer - he said he would not answer any more questions without the advice of his solicitor, as it might implicate himself - he then asked what was the matter about this wool - Mr. Rayson said it had been stolen - the prisoner asked if it belonged to him - he said "No," he represented the person to whom it belonged - he said he did not wish anybody to lose any thing by him - that he bought it very dear, and should not get a penny a pound by it; and he would pay the value, and pay the officer's expenses - Mr. Rayson said he could not hold out any promise, or undertake any thing of that kind - in going along in the coach, Mr. Bell said the wool had gone a long way into the country; but he did not say where - after he was in custody, I went on the Friday morning into the neighbourhood of his house, and in consequence of information, I went to Pickford's wharf, and from thence to Huddersfield, where I found this wool in a boat - I searched the prisoner's house the night I took him, and there were several pieces of canvass, which I took up and put on a chair till the morning - I then went again, and they were gone; but I found some sweepings of wool, which I have here - I searched the prisoner to see if he had any documents of wool going away, and he had not.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. When did you go to search the prisoner? A. On the Friday, at Clerkenwell prison, to see if he had any papers relative to the wool which had been sent to Pickford's - that was all I went for- I did not go there to see what I could get out of him - I had no such intention - Mr. Rayson and Mr. Smith were with me - we were going to Pickford's in the early part of the morning, and we called there first - I did not go to ask him any questions, nor I do not believe the others did - there was some conversation between Mr. Bell and Mr. Smith - I did not put any question to him - I know my duty better than that - the reason I had not searched him before he was in prison was, I had not discovered where the wool was - I did not ask him if there was any body in the neighbourhood to whom he could refer me - I did not ask him about Mr. Marson - I heard Mr. Marson's name mentioned, but I do not recollect by whom - I went to Mr. Marson in consequence of something I heard - I will swear I did not ask the prisoner where to find Mr. Marson - I know nothing of Mr. Marson - the prisoner did mention something about Mr. Marson - he talked a great deal.
Q. Do you recollect the words the prisoner made use of when he came to his home? A. I asked if his name was Bell - he said "Yes" - I then said I came about a bale of wool that had been brought to his house yesterday - I did not give him any other description of the article that was lost - I did not say German skin wool - if I had used such words, I must have recollected it - I did not produce my staff - if Mr. Rayson has sworn I did, he must be mistaken - there was a great deal of confusion at the time with Mr. Bell- I went to Mr. Marson's, but I think Mr. Rayson went before I did - I made some inquiries in the neighbourhood.
JOSEPH MARSON. I am a wool-broker, and live in Basinghall-street. I know the prisoner - I saw him on the 12th of February, about one o'clock - he came to me as I was in the White Hart, Bell Alley, taking dinner - he produced a small sample of German skin wool, and wished me to purchase it - he asked 2s. 2d. a pound for it - I objected to that price - he then offered it at 2s. 1d. - I said I was not a buyer at all - he then asked if I would take it at 2s. - I said the market was very flat, and I would have nothing to do with it - I saw him again the next day, and he said he had sent the wool off on speculation - he said he had about 5 cwt. of it - I have seen the wool produced by the officer, it was of a similar description to this.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Does wool change its colour? A. Not particularly - I have known the prisoner four or five years - there was no appearance of concealment in this matter - it is impossible to tell the price of German skin wool - it differs in every sample - when I saw this wool at Lambeth-street office, I thought it worth 2s. 6d., a pound - it has now fallen considerably - I saw the sample the prisoner produced - I do not know that that was the same - 1s. 10d., was certainly a very reasonable price, for what I saw - if I had wanted wool, I should have had no objection to have bought of the prisoner.
JOHN BARNETT. I am a clerk to Messrs. John Cooper and Spratt. We received nine bales of wool which had been conveyed from Hamburgh - we ought to have received 10 - this is the bill of lading - the Nos. are No. 201, 202, 203, 204, 205, 206, 207, 208, 209, 210; seven of these were marked with a triangle, and loops at the end, and three with a plain triangle - we only received six with a triangle and loops to it - the number of the one missing was 207 - the weight of that was 535lbs. Hamburgh weight, but we generally add about 7 per cent. to the Hamburgh weight which would be 35lbs. more - here is a piece on one of these bags which seems to correspond with the wrapper of the following number - we received the bill of lading from Mr. Forgess, on the 11th of February, with a desire that we should enter them, and clear them from the Custom-house- I made an entry at the Custom-house, and the wools were landed from the vessel - the endorsement on this bill is Mr. Barr's - we expected ten bales. and we received nine- if there should have been only landed at Galley Quay, six with the triangles and circles, and three with the triangles only that would be nine, the exact number we received - if six and three were all that were landed, that would be all we could receive.
WILLIAM AUSTIN FOSSETT. I am a lighterman. On the 11th of February I had a lug-boat moored at Galley Quay in the evening, and on the following morning I missed it - I had given no permission to any one to take it away - she was afterwards found at Limehouse abandoned - she was a lug-boat of about twelve tons burden - she would hold a dozen bales of wool, or more than that - my name was on it, and the number, and "Galley Quay."
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were the words"Legal quay," on your boat? A. No.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What were the words? A."William Fossett, Galley Quay, No. 7." - Galley Quay is a legal quay - it is known as such.
JOHN CLEMENTS. I am in the employ of Mr. Fossett. I went in search of his boat, and found her a short distance from Limehouse-cut, on the 13th of February.
MR. BODKIN to JOHN BARNETT. Q. Do you know whether any other bales were landed for you the day afterwards? A. Yes, I saw them landed - my evidence applied to the total number we received from the Belfast.
WILLIAM JOYCE re-examined. Q. How many bales from the Belfast were landed on the 11th? A. Five with the triangles and circles, and some the day afterwards.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What time on the next day were they landed? A. About one or two o'clock- I did not see them taken away - there were two came the following day - I did state on my examination before that on the 11th of February, only fourteen were landed.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Have you any notes? A. Yes; these are them; here is eight on one, and seven on the other - I did not before swear there were only fourteen.
JOSEPH DUFTON. I am a woollen-manufacturer at Leeds. I did not expect any wool from the prisoner on the 11th of February - I had no advice of it.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know Mr. Lupton? A. Yes; I should have taken wool from him at any time, if it suited me.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Should you have expected some advice? A. Yes, and I had no advice of any at that time.
COURT. Q. Do you receive from some of your correspondents goods without advice? A. No; I never did in my life from anybody, without I had some advice of them - I did not receive two bags within a week of the time these were sent without any advice - I have not received any thing from Mr. Lupton this year - I think I did in December - he sent the wool down, and it laid there a week - he then sent me advice that if I liked it at a certain price, I might keep it.( Abraham Riddiford, woollen-draper, Basinghall-street; James Chamberlain, Ball's-pond; Mark Hagar, slopseller, Bath-street, St. Luke's; Jeremiah Ramplin, coffee-house keeper, Shoreditch; James Jiggers, a draper, Newcastle-street, Strand; James James, tailor, Provost-street, City-road; Joseph Ladbroke, woollen-draper, No. 60, Wood-street, Cheapside; Mrs. Underwood; Lydia Anderson, Suffolk-street, Cornwall-road; Susan Rumball, Radnor-street; William Nash, furrier, Southwark-bridge; Hannah Hall, Aldersgate-street, and Maria Ward, Eagle Inn, Gravesend, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 53. - Recommended to mercy by the Jury. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18340410-196]
718. PETER BARTLETT was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of February, 10 pair of gloves, value 1l. 5s., and 6 pair of unfinished gloves, value 12s., the goods of George Arnold, his master.
GEORGE ARNOLD. I live in Berners-street, and am a glove-manufacturer. The prisoner had been my journeyman three or four months - I missed gloves, which induced me to mark two or three dozen, both finished and unfinished - I put these on the counter on the 27th of February, in the morning - the prisoner came in and stood at that counter - I missed half-a-dozen pairs - the officer stood at the door, and followed him when he went out at dinner-time.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I am an officer. I was waiting at the prosecutor's door, and followed the prisoner to Tabernacle-walk - he went into No. 5, and in four or five minutes he came out, and went into a public-house - I took him in charge, and took six pair of gloves out of each of his coat pockets, and this dozen from his hat - he said they were his own.
MR. ARNOLD. These are my gloves - they are what I marked.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. What is the mark? A. This is it; "six not welted" - I never received any gloves of the prisoner in my life - he said two or three times that he had two duplicates of gloves, which he bought of a little manufacturer, who had broken, and I purchased them of him - I went into a public-house with the prisoner on the morning he was taken, on our road home - we had some porter, but no gin - I said to him, "Bartlett, I am sorry for you" - my house has not been searched for stolen goods.
CHARLES LONGMAN. I am journeyman to the prosecutor. The prisoner worked there - he told me if I would take some gloves, he could sell them, and he could put me up to more than I knew - he came on the Monday morning to work, and took two skins - I told my master, and he told me to hold on with him, and try if I could pick out where he sold them - he came again on the Tuesday, and took a pair of gloves which were finished; and on Wednesday morning he took three pair, which were not finished, and said he was going to sell them at dinner-time.
Cross-examined. Q. He told you he could put you up to something? A. Yes, on the Saturday night - I had only been there a few days - I had before lived at Yeovill with my brother-in-law, and worked for Mr. Wilmington - there was something occurred between us; but he gave me a very good character, and gave me up to Mr. Cook - I was not discharged for having received money for goods, and entering them in the book as not paid for - I received the money, and gave it to my master's daughter - I was then called down to tea, and was told I had received half-a-crown more than I had paid; but I had not.(The prisoner put in a written defence, declaring his innocence.)
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Confined for Six Months.
[trialAccount t18340410-197]
719. JOHN WILLS was indicted for receiving, on the 26th of February, 4 pair of gloves, value 10s., the goods of George Arnold, well knowing them to have been stolen.
GEORGE ARNOLD. This prisoner lived in Tabernacle-walk - I followed the last prisoner to his house; and after we took Bartlett, we went to this prisoner's house, and found four pair of gloves on the table and in the drawer, which are mine - the prisoner is a glove-manufacturer - he said he bought those gloves of Bartlett.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I am an officer. I went with Mr. Arnold to the prisoner's house - I said to the prisoner, "I believe you had Bartlett here yesterday?" - he said, "No"- I said, "You have to-day?" - he said "No" - I said,"I am witness that he came here to-day" - Mr. Arnold then found four pair of gloves, and said, "These four pair I had in my hand yesterday - I can swear to them" - the prisoner then said, "I had them of Peter Bartlett."
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. He keeps a glove shop? A. Yes; some of these were in his window - I do not know whether he was at home when Bartlett went to his house. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340410-198]
720. THOMAS SMITH, WILLIAM HINCHLEY, RICHARD HIBBERT, and JAMES LUTMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, three sacks, value 3s.; and 12 bushels of wheat, value 4l.; the goods of William Burr, the master of the said Thomas Smith, William Hinchley, and Richard Hibbert.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES BURR. I am in the employ of William Burr; he is a miller, and lives at Hillingdon. The prisoners Smith, Hinchley, and Hibbert were in his employ: on the 5th of April, I was in the upper floor of the mill - I could see the wheat bin, and the wheat was running out - I called George Clarke, and we went down the ladder to the lower floor - I saw Hinchley and Smith taking the wheat down - Hibbert had got hold of a truck; they put the sacks of wheat on the truck, and took it to Lutman's cart, which was backed up against the door - we could not see the cart from there, but we went to another place, five or six feet off - we then saw them putting the wheat into Lutman's cart - they put in three sacks of wheat, and a sack of bran, Lutman was with the cart - my master had left about eleven o'clock that day, to go to Windsor - Lutman left with the cart a little before one o'clock - I went to dinner, and passed by the Fox, at Cowley, where I saw Lutman's cart standing with the four sacks in it.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How did you see them? A. Through a hole in the next floor, about as large as a pane of glass - the bin was on the side of the floor - we were in the floor over it - we kneeled down - we knew they were stealing it - the bin holds about four loads - my master does not sell wheat - the mill feeds from that bin - I was not there in the old foreman's time - Mr. Clarke is foreman now - I do not know of any dislike between Mr. Clarke and the prisoners - we could not see the bin, and the proceedings at the cart at the same time - I ran to another hole - I was on the second floor from the ground - Hinchley was holding a sack, Smith was holding a bag, and Hibbert wheeled it to the cart - Lutman was against the door - there were three holes; through one hole I could see the wheat running: through another hole I could see the cart - I went to one hole, and then to another.
Q. Recollect yourself, you say you went to a hole in the loft, and saw from that into the yard, and saw Lutman and his cart, how did you do that? A. I ran to another hole, and then saw them putting it into the cart - we looked through a board - Mr. Clarke has not been tutoring me what to say - he spoke to me about this on the Monday morning, before I had been to the Justice, and the day before yesterday he did not tell me what I was to say - he asked me whether it was the truth, that I did see them, and I said yes.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you say correctly at first, that after you looked through the first floor, you descended a ladder, and looked through another hole? A. Yes; the bin is very large, the hopper is in the middle - the bag is at one corner - the wheat was not running from the middle where the hopper is, but from the corner where the bag is.
GEORGE CLARKE. I am the son of John Clarke, he is foreman of the prosecutor's mill. I was in the mill on the 5th of April, with Charles Burr, between twelve and one o'clock - we were at the top of the mill - there is a hole there - I looked through, and saw some wheat running out from the bin, from one corner where there was a bag - we then went down to the next floor, and looked through a hole, and saw the wheat run down into a sack; and the three prisoners who are employed in the mill, were there - we saw three sacks, which were put into Lutman's cart - we could see the door from the hole we looked through the second time - Hibbert wheeled the sack to Lutman's cart, and put them in - I told my father when he came home at night.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Why did not you tell your father when he came home to dinner? A. I do not know - I have not talked with my father above once or twice about them - he and the prisoners have had some quarrels - I told my father as I went home at night, and again the next day - when we first saw the prisoners, we were at the top of the mill - the hole was about as large as a square of glass - we looked through to the floor under us, and saw wheat running - the top of the bin is not inclosed by a sack.
Q. Was the hole in the under floor immediately under the hole in the top floor? A. Yes; there was nothing to prevent our seeing through the two floors at once - I saw through the first hole the hopper, and then I came down to see through the other hole - the hole in the room where the bin was, is about five yards from the bin - I know the difference between a foot and a yard - it was about five yards.
Q. Have you not been talking about five somethings, and forgotten whether it was five feet or five yards? A. No; it was five yards - there was no other cart in the road at that time - I know Beech - he came with his cart at the time Lutman went away - my father talked with Charles Burr about this business the next day before we went before the magistrate - my father talked to me once after this about it, but not in Burr's presence - he told me he had talked with Burr - I am sure of that - I do not know what quarrel my father has had with these prisoners - they have been disputing - my father has rather less wages than some of them - he is not jealous of them - they said something to offend him about three weeks before this, and they had quarrelled about a month before - my father is rather angry with them - I was at Clerkenwell with my father before the Grand Jury - we talked on the road from Uxbridge - he has not talked over and over again with me about it.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you quarrelled with the prisoners? A. No; the bran is not kept in the place where the wheat is.
JOHN CLARKE. I am foreman to Mr. Burr. On the 5th April I was at the Fox public-house - I saw Lutman there with his cart - I do not know what was in it - my son gave me some information when I went home at night - I examined the corn bin - two or three sacks of corn were gone - I know the hole the boy showed me in the floor - I could see what they described.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is there a hole in the top floor? A. No; not in the boards - down the ladder you can see into the second floor - there is no holein the boards in the floor at the top, that you can see into the second floor - there is no floor above where the bin is - it would be impossible for any one to look through a floor above where the bin is, and see it.
Q. How many floors are there? A. Four - the bin is on the fourth floor - it would be necessary for a person in the third floor, to look through a hole to see the wheat flow into the bin - there is no hole in a floor exactly under another hole: but within sight of it, three or four yards from it.
Q. Have you been speaking to any of the witnesses in this case? A. No; not on any particular point - I did not speak to any one about what he was to say - I only told my son to tell the truth - I never knew him to tell a story - I spoke to my son to-day about it - I told him to tell the truth.
Q. Did you go before the Grand Jury with him? A. No; I did not - I came from Uxbridge with him - I do not know whether I talked with him there - I have been foreman at the mill two years - I found two of these prisoners there when I went there - I have 100l. a-year - Hinchly has 27s. a-week - Hibbert 20s., and Smith 23s. - I have had no quarrel with these men in particular, only in the course of work - I have ordered them what to do - they have insulted me many times, but I never resented it nor cared for it - I never told my boy of it - he might have heard my wife speak of it - I have never spoken to Charles Burr, or my son about this, but only to tell the truth.
WILLIAM BURR. I am the proprietor of the mill. I know the situation - if these boys were in the upper part of the mill, they could see the bin, and by coming down they could see the wheat running into the sacks, and by removing a little on one side they could see the door.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you often been in the mill? A. Yes; but I never knew of these holes till this transaction - I cannot tell whether they were made for this purpose - I should think none were so large as a foot - there are holes in many large buildings - three of these men have been many years in my employ, long before Clarke came.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340410-199]
Fifth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
721. JOHN JOHNSON was indicted for bigamy.
MARY ANN BOTELAR. I live at No. 68, Charlotte-street, Portland Place. I am single. I know the prisoner - I was present when he was married at St. Andrew's, Holborn, on the 14th of October, 1827 - he married Mary Ann Harvey - I was her bridesmaid - I knew the prisoner before that time, and Mary Ann Harvey also - she is now alive; I saw her to-day - I am positive the prisoner and she were the persons who were then married.
MARY ROBERTS. I am the mother of Mary Ann Roberts - I know the prisoner - he used to come to see my daughter, and he married her at All-Souls church, on the 15th of September last - I was not at the church, but I saw the certificate of the marriage - they came to my house and passed as man and wife from that time - they lived together.
Prisoner. Q. How do you understand that I married your daughter? Witness. I was not present, but I heard them asked in the church.
ROBERT BUGG. I am beadle of Clerkenwell. Mary Ann Roberts was before the magistrate, but she is under age, and her mother was bound for her appearance - I produce these two certificates - I have examined them with the books - they state that "John Johnson of this parish and Mary Ann Harvey of this parish were married in this church by banns, on this 14th day of October, 1827," and that "John Johnson, bachelor, and Mary Ann Roberts, of the district rectory of All-Souls, in this parish, spinster, were married in this church, by banns, on the 15th day of September, 1833" - I know the prisoner by his having relief from our parish - his wife pointed him out as her husband.
GUILTY. Aged 29. - Confined for One Year.
[trialAccount t18340410-200]
722. JOHN JAMES FRASER was indicted for bigamy, and that he was afterwards apprehended in the county of Middlesex.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES KNIGHT. I am a plumber and glazier. I produce these certificates, one from Queenborough in Kent, and the other from St. George the Martyr - they are true copies. (Read.)
"Marriages solemnized in the parish of Queenborough, in the county of Kent, 1830 - John James Fraser, widower, of this parish, and Sarah Dunn, widow, of this parish, were married in this church by banns, this 25th day of November, in the year 1830, &c."
"Marriages solemnized in the parish of St. George the Martyr, Southwark, in the county of Surrey, in the year 1831. - James Fraser, of this parish, widower, and Ann Bliss, of this parish, widow, were married in this church by banns, this 21st day of December, in the year 1831, &c."
FRANCES GREEN. I am married, and live at Sheerness - in November, 1830, I was present at the marriage of the prisoner with Sarah Dunn - I witnessed the register - the prisoner is the man.
Cross-examined by MR. STAMMERS. Q. You are sure it was the prisoner? A. Yes, I had never known him before then - he was married in the name of James Fraser.
MR. DOANE. Q. When did you last see Sarah Dunn? A. A little while back - I have seen her several times since 1831.
MR. STAMMERS. Q. But you had not seen her before that? A. No - I cannot tell whether her name was Sarah Johnson or what it was - Sarah Dunn was the name she went by on that occasion, and I heard several persons speak of the marriage afterwards.
COURT. Q. But afterwards, when you heard the people talk of a marriage, did they talk of Fraser having married Sarah Dunn? A. Yes, and she was married by that name.
MARY ANN BLISS. I lived in Lambeth in 1831, and carried on business there - on the 21st of December, 1831, I married the prisoner in the parish church of St. George the Martyr, Southwark, in the name of Ann Bliss - my name is Mary Ann Bliss - I gave my name so to Mr. Fraser, but on the day of our marriage he told me it was only a mistake made by the clerk.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you never hear of this mistake before the day of the marriage? A. No, I did not - Inever gave Fraser only one name - he told me in the church that it was a mistake of the clerk, he had put "Ann" and omitted to put "Mary," but that was of no consequence at all, and I did not think it was - he never told me of it before - I had been married before - I gave my right name to my first husband, it was Mary Ann Archbold.
Q. And were you not quite aware that you should give your full name? A. I did so to Mr. Fraser - I was married by banns in the first case - I supposed it was necessary that the banns should be published in my right name - I was never present but at one marriage in my life - it was not proposed that I should be married by Dr. Doyle - I never went to any Roman Catholic chapel to be married.
JAMES COOKE (police-sergeant H 7). I apprehended the prisoner in Grove-street, Commercial-road, in the county of Middlesex.
Prisoner's Defence. I have been in confinement nearly nine months. On the 5th of September last I was tried, and my trial was condemned by the twelve judges, and I was to be discharged on the 26th of February, but was liable to be tried again in Surrey - the first time I knew Mrs. Bliss, I lived in Westminster - at her request I went to live with her - it was her own persuasion, and through her own wish and request we both got married in wrong names, and she told her brother she was married before we were married.
GUILTY. Aged 40. - Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18340410-201]
723. CAROLINE JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 4 shirts, value 2l.; 6 cravats, value 5s.; 7 pair of socks, value 7s.; 2 flannel petticoats, value 4s.; 1 handkerchief, value 2s.; 3 gowns, value 2l.; 1 shift, value 3s.; 2 petticoats, value 10s.; 2 waistcoats, value 3s.; 4 pair of stockings, value 8s.; 4 collars, value 15s.; 1 waistcoat, value 1s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 1s., the goods of Theodore Zimmerman: 5 caps, value 3s.; 1 collar, value 6d.; and 2 pair of stockings, value 1s., the goods of Catherine Slater, and that she had been before convicted of felony; and WILLIAM JOHN JONES was indicted for feloniously receiving on the same day, 1 handkerchief, value 2s.; 1 waistcoat, value 1s. 6d.; one petticoat, value 8s.; 2 gowns, value 2s.; 3 pair of stockings, value 6s.; and 1 cap, value 6d.; being part and parcel of the said goods, well knowing the same to have been feloniously stolen, &c., against the Statute.
CATHERINE SLATER. I live in Norfolk-street, Strand. Mr. Theodore Zimmerman lodged in the house, but he is now abroad - on the 10th of February Caroline Jones came there, and said she came from Mrs. Farmer, our washerwoman, for the linen - I asked if she had any order from the washerwoman - she said "No," but she had begged of her to come and not to leave the house without the things - I told Mrs. Zimmerman - she looked up the articles stated for her - I gave them to her: and in coming down stairs, I told her to wait, and I got my own linen for her - she pushed them all into the basket, and took them away - I felt quite sure she came from there - the washerwoman is in the habit of coming herself.
MARY PALMER. I wash for Mrs. Zimmerman and Catherine Slater. I never sent Caroline Jones for any of this linen - I never saw her till she was in custody.
REYNA SYMONS. I live in Three-tun-alley, Petticoat-lane. On the 10th of February, Caroline Jones told me she had a few things to sell for a widow - I bought three shirts, four lace collars, one gown, one waistcoat, a pair of drawers, and one or two other things - I gave her 16s. in money, and 9s. worth of earthen-ware, out of my basket - I sold them the next day for 31s. to Mrs. Cheetham.
MARY CHEETHAM. I bought these articles of Symons for 31s.
ROBERT BROOKS (police-constable G 29). I apprehended the prisoners in Somers Town - my brother officer found some of the property, which is here - Caroline Jones wished us to be quiet, and not let the neighbours hear any thing of it - she asked us to call her husband down stairs - my brother officer called down the other prisoner, and when they were both in the room, my brother officer laid hold of this basket of linen - Caroline Jones said, "You need not trouble that: that is mine" - my brother officer then took up this handkerchief - she said, "You need not trouble that: that is my husband's;" and the male prisoner said,"That is mine" - my brother officer said, "If that is yours, that is the first article I shall take" - we found the rest of these articles in a basket in the house - I had before been to Mrs. Cheetham's, in St. John-street, and found these articles which she had bought.
EDMUND LANCASTER (police-constable G 197). I went with Brooks to the house - I called the male prisoner down - he said the handkerchief was his.
THOMAS YAPP. The prisoners rent a house of me, No. 7, Smith's-place, Somers Town - I believe they took it together - they represented themselves as man and wife - the man said he was a ladies' shoemaker.
COURT to E. LANCASTER. Q. Did you ask this woman if her husband was at home? A. Yes, and she said he was up stairs - I went up to him, and saw him at work with two lads - I told him I wanted him about some boots; and when he came down, I told him my business.
JAMES GLIBBERY (police-serjeant N 21). I produce a certificate of the conviction of Caroline Jones, which I got from Mr. Clark's office - I know her to be the person.(The prisoner, Caroline Jones, put in a written defence, stating that her husband and a Jewess had induced her to commit the offence; and that her husband had treated her with great brutality, in consequence of her refusing to support him by dishonest acts.)
William John Jones. It is utterly false.
JAMES SMITH. I live with my mother in Brewer-street, Somers Town - I was employed in sewing shoes at the prisoner's house - I have heard him tell his wife to go out, and if she said "No," he said, "Do go this time, there is 10s. to pay for rent, and I have had a very short week" - I heard him say so on the Monday before the 10th of February - Caroline Jones is my sister.
CAROLINE JONES - GUILTY.* Aged 20.
Confined Ten Months.
WILLIAM JOHN JONES - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340410-217]
724. JOSEPH HALEY was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d., the goods of William Thompson, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM THOMPSON. I live in Rood-lane. On the 9th of March, about eleven o'clock in the morning, I was walking along Chiswell-street - I received information and missed my handkerchief, which I had had a few minutes before - I took the prisoner, and gave him in charge - it was a red silk handkerchief.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Has it been found? A. No - I did not see any body run - a gentleman was with me, but he is not here - I was near Finsbury-square at the time - I took the prisoner close to me.
EDWARD WILLIAMS. I am clerk at a Manchester warehouse, in Wood-street, Cheapside - I was in Chiswell-street, and saw the prisoner and another following the prosecutor and his friend - I thought the prisoner was making too familiar with the prosecutor's coat - I saw him lift the tail of it up, and then take out the handkerchief - it was a red one, but I was not near enough to observe whether it was silk or cotton - I gave notice to the prosecutor - he turned and took hold of the prisoner, who was not half a yard from him - when the prisoner took the handkerchief out, he passed it behind him towards his companion, who was on the left side of him.
Cross-examined. Q. How near were you to the prisoner? A. About twenty yards - I was on the other side of the way - it wanted about twenty minutes to eleven o'clock - I crossed over and told the prosecutor - I did not see what became of the other man - I did not see any one run away - I said I supposed the prisoner might have put it into his pocket - I saw him take the handkerchief - if I had looked after the other man, as well as the prisoner, I might not have taken one of them - the street was full of people.
ROBERT STEWART (City police-constable, No. 54). I was called, and the prisoner was given into my custody - I found nothing on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going to my aunt's to dinner - the witness came across the road, touched the prosecutor and said, "This man has taken your handkerchief out of your pocket" - I said, "You are mistaken;" but the prosecutor said, "I certainly must give more credence to this gentleman than to you" - I was taken - nothing was found on me.
ABRAHAM HART. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark 's office - I know him to be the man.
GUILTY.* Aged 23. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18340410-202]
OLD COURT. - Wednesday, April 14th.
First Middlesex Jury, Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
725. JOHN PAGE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 80 yards of silk, value 12l., of James Shudi Broadwood, and another, in their dwelling-house.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON. I am a policeman. On the 5th of April, I was on duty in Marylebone-lane, and saw the prisoner in company with another man, (not Willard), he had a roll of something under his arm, and was talking to Moss, a Jew, who keeps a clothes-shop - in consequence of what Moss told me, I watched them to the shop of Barnett, a clothes-dealer, then to the shop of Lazarus - the prisoner still had the goods under his arm - I accosted him, and asked, what he had got there? - he said a piece of silk - I asked if it was his own - he said it was - I asked what he was? - he said, a porter - I asked who to - he said he was a job porter - I said I had seen him go to several shops, which I thought was not right, and asked what colour the silk was? - he said he believed it was red- I asked if he knew how many yards there were - he said he did not - I took hold of him, and the other man, who was afterwards discharged - in going to the station-house, I asked where he lived? - he said, at Bayswater - at the station-house, I asked if he really lived at Bayswater? - he said, "Yes," and he would tell me all about it - I told him to reserve what he had to say, for the magistrate - he said, he was at work at Messrs. Broadwood's, and a man named Willard, who was an under clerk, had given him the silk to sell - he produced a piece of paper Willard had given him, to make a return on it, as to what the silk sold for.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. He gave you Willard's address? A. Yes; I afterwards saw Willard, and made him write his name on a piece of paper, to see if the handwriting was the same, and it was.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did he say Willard called on him to sell the silk, or what? A. He said, Willard gave it him to sell - nothing was said about calling on him - Willard is an under store keeper at Broadwood's; and the prisoner was a French polisher, employed there.
FREDERICK SNOOK. I am a tailor, and live in Wigmore-street, at Mr. Romanell's. The prisoner called at Mr. Romanell's shop, on Friday evening, and told me he had a piece of silk to dispose of, and he wanted clothes in exchange, part in clothes, and part in money - he brought the silk - I showed it our shopman, who told me to have nothing at all to do with it.
THOMAS AYLING. I am in the employ of Messrs. James Shudi and Thomas Broadwood; the house of business is in Pulteny-street, St. James's, Westminster - I cannot swear to this silk, but I know the wrapper it is in. The prisoner was employed about Messrs. Broadwood's premises, as a French-polisher, under his uncle, not as their servant.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known him? A. About four years - I know nothing dishonest of him.
HENRY HENMAN. I am in the employ of Harding and Smith, silk-mercers, Pall Mall. This is one of three pieces of silk which I sent to Broadwood's on the 25th of March - it is a double sarsnet, used by pianoforte makers, and is wider than is generally used - there are eighty yards of it, worth 12l. - this is the wrapper I sent the silk in to Broadwood's - and the mark on the block of the silk corresponds with the mark on the paper.
Prisoner's Defence. On Friday, Willard came into the shop at Mr. Broadwood's, and said he wanted to speak to me. I went to the bottom of the stairs. He said he had a piece of silk given him by the gentleman (who served Messrs. Broadwood with silk), as a Christmas-box, and he had had it some time - he asked if I knew who was likely to buy it. I said, "No," but if it was his own, and he came honestly by it, the Jews would soon purchase it - he said, "As you go home, you pass several shops, would yougo and inquire?" I said I would; and, as I went home, I called at the Jew's, and said, I knew a person who had silk to dispose of - he said, "Let me see it; if it suits, I will buy it." On Saturday, Willard asked if I had spoken to any body? I said, "Yes, and they would look at it"- he brought the silks in his hand to the pay table, in Windmill-street, about half-past six o'clock. I went with him to a public-house - he asked the bar-maid to give him a pen and ink - he wrote me the direction where I was to call. I was not to ask any price for it, but to go, and let him know if it suited the party, and he would call himself, and arrange the price. I went to the shop about the silk, and directly I opened it, I thought it was not his own. The Jew said, if it was gown silk, he would buy it - I was bringing it away, and when I got to Marylebone, (Proctor, my shopmate, was with me,) a Jew caught hold of my arm, and asked if I wanted to buy any thing? - then the policeman caught hold of me, and asked what I had? I said, "Silk." I said it was not my own. I said I was a polisher - I did not say I was a porter - at the station-house I said, "I am drawn into this, and dare say I shall suffer for it, although innocent."
BROOK PAYNE. I am the prisoner's uncle. He is in my employ - I am a French-polisher - Willard is in Mr. Broadwood's employ, and is called under-foreman, I believe - I remember seeing him on Saturday, the 5th of April - he had a paper parcel in his hand - a light-brown parcel, from ten to eighteen inches - I spoke to him - Willard was in the habit of going into my shop to fetch young men out, to go on errands - the prisoner has been in my employ five years, and always attended to my business.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Willard was employed at Broadwood's? A. Yes; and my nephew was employed on their premises by me - I was employed there by contract - I know they use silk in their business, but I cannot say it was silk - it was a paper parcel that I saw - I never knew a mercer give away silk as a Christmas-box - it might be so for all I know.
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON re-examined. I produce the paper the prisoner gave me.
JURY. Q. When you took the prisoner was the piece of silk rolled up in the same manner as it is now? A. Exactly the same.
JURY to T. AYLING. Q. Could a person employed under another have access to the silk, he being a French-polisher? A. Not without my consent or Willard's, who had access to it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340410-203]
726. WILLIAM STEWARD was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 1 ham, value 6s., the goods of Frederick Pamphilon.
FREDERICK PAMPHILON. I keep a cheese-shop in Skinner-street, Somers Town. On the 27th of March, while I was standing behind the counter, I saw the glimpse of two men pass the window, and heard a voice say, "Tom, here's a ham" - my man ran out, and the ham was gone - one of the policemen took the prisoner.
THOMAS MITCHELL. I am a policeman. The prisoner passed by me with the ham under his coat - he went into a dark court, and my brother officer took him - I found the ham in a dark passage where he went down, and dropped it - we secured him as he came out.
HENRY MARTIN. I am a policeman. I was standing at the corner of Cromer-street - the prisoner passed with a ham partly concealed under his coat - I followed and took him into custody - the ham was found two yards from him.
Prisoner. I hope for mercy.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18340410-204]
727. RICHARD SMITH was indicted was stealing, on the 20th of March, 40lbs. of lead, value 5s.; the goods of Robert Briggs and another, and fixed to a building, against the Statute, &c.
JOHN SHEPHERD. I am a policeman. On the 20th of March, about ten minutes after nine o'clock in the evening I was in Cock-alley, Norton-falgate - I saw a boy standing at the back of Briggs's premises - I suspected him - another joined him soon after, and when they saw us, they made off- neither of them was the prisoner - I sent the constable, and took the two boys into custody - I found a piece of lead where the boys stood - I went up stairs in a house in Cock-alley, in half an hour - I found the prisoner in bed, and this piece of lead lying about two yards from him; another boy was in bed with him - we took them both into custody - they said they knew nothing of the lead - it was concealed under the cinders in the garret - we took five persons who were in the house into custody - next morning I fitted the lead to Briggs's premises, and it tallied exactly; also the piece which I found where the boys stood - I took them all five before the magistrate - they were all discharged except the prisoner - I was present at the examination - I saw the magistrate sign the deposition which was read over to the prisoner - no threat or promise was held out to him - this is Mr. Broughton's handwriting (read) -"At the second examination, the prisoner said, the other persons knew nothing about the lead, for that he found it, and carried it there."
ALEXANDER SCOTT. I am a policeman. Shepherd's evidence is correct.
THOMAS BREESE. I am foreman to Robert Briggs and another - the back of their premises opens into Cock-alley - I had seen the lead secure on the top of the manufactory before this happened - the party had got on the roof from the outside - it is a great height - they must have had a ladder to get up - the lead appeared quite bright and fresh - there is a soot shed adjoining the premises - they must have got on the top of the shed; the lead was cut in a very unworkmanlike manner - I have seen the prisoner about the neighbourhood.
Prisoner's Defence. About eight o'clock at night, I went to bed - I went outside to do what I wanted, and saw the lead lying on the ruins - I took it up stairs and went to bed.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340410-205]
728. THOMAS WILLIAM TOTTENHAM PRESCOTT was indicted for feloniously forging an order for the payment of 50l., with intent to defraud Richard Henry Cox and others.
2nd COUNT for uttering the same with the like intent.
Other Counts, varying the manner of stating the charge.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES POWELL. I am clerk to Richard Henry Cox and two others. On the 14th of December last, I saw the prisoner at our house - we had a correspondent at that time, named Captain Gordon - the prisoner produced this letter to me - I asked him to favour me with his name - he said his name was Wainwright - I said, "Captain Wainwright?" - he answered, "Yes;" and added, "Not Captain Wainwright, late of the 47th" - I then told him, as the order was to pay the money on the sale of the stock, I could not pay it him then; but that he must come again in a few days' time, when the stock would be sold - he asked what day he should come - I think Wednesday was mentioned - this was on a Saturday - he remonstrated at not being paid then, and stated that he had received the order from Captain Gordon for a particular purpose, and that it was of a pressing nature - I said I was sorry to put him to inconvenience, but I could not pay the money till the stock was sold - he then left - I saw him again on the 18th of December; I think it was Wednesday (it was a few days after this) - he asked if I was ready then to pay the money - I said, "Yes," and asked him his Christain name - he replied, "Charles" - I then filled up the order, conceiving he was the proper person for whom it was intended, and whom he represented himself to be - this is the order (looking at it) - he signed it in my presence - this order which he signed was the authority for paying the money - in the course of our business, the party applying would be called on to sign such an order as this before it was paid - we do not pay the money in my department of the house - we should not part with the money unless that cheque had been signed.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Have you ever seen Captain Gordon since he wrote that letter? A. No; I know nothing of the prisoner except in this transaction - we were agents for Captain Wainwright, late of the 47th- Captain Gordon is also of the 47th - Captain Wainwright's name is not Charles - we were agents for him while he was in the 47th - he is on half-pay now, and not in any regiment.
Q. The prisoner brought you that letter which was your voucher or authority for payment of this money? A. Yes, on his signing the cheque; the letter was my authority to charge it to Captain Gordon's account - if Captain Gordon asked for a voucher, I should have to produce the letter and the receipt - the letter is my authority, supported by the receipt.
Q. Do you use and treat that paper which you have produced in any other way than a receipt for the payment of money, which Captain Gordon had previously ordered to be paid? A. I treat it as a draft in acknowledgment of the money - Captain Charles Wainwright, if there is such a person, had no money in our hands to give an order to receive - the letter does not mention any Christain name - I am not aware that there is such a person as Charles Wainwright, who has any money in our hands to which an order could apply - I do not know all the clients of the house - I paid the money on the faith of Captain Gordon's letter to the party who said he was Captain Wainwright - Captain Gordon is at Mullingar - he corresponds with us from Ireland.
MR. BODKIN. Q. The letter of Captain Gordon was the authority on which you sold out his stock? A. It was; the cashier pays the money on authority of the draft - he does not see the original order, when he pays the money - he pays it on production of the draft which the prisoner signed - my handwriting is on the draft.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. The cashier knows your handwriting on the draft? A. Yes; my initials are on it - that forms a voucher or authority to the cashier to pay the money - he would not pay it unless it was marked by the clerk, who has charge of the account.
COURT. Q. If the draft was not signed, your initials alone would not be the authority to pay it? A. No; the draft must be signed before the cashier would pay it.
JURY. Q. Did you consider you were paying the money to Captain Wainwright late of the 47th? A. No: I did not pay the money - I knew Captain Wainwright, late of the 47th, and supposed the prisoner to be another Captain Wainwright.
FREDERICK WEEDON. I am cashier to Messrs. Cox and Co. I paid this draft on the day it bears date, from the funds of Cox and Co.
WILLIAM BALLARD. I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner on the Wednesday preceding the examination - I do not remember the day of the month - I took him in Titchbourn-street, Coventry-street - I knew him as Prescott - I met him accidentally in the street - he made a stop and said, "How do you do?" - I said, "I had been looking for you some time, I want you" - he said, "For what?" - I said,"I have a warrant against you" - he said, "What is it for?" - I said, "For obtaining money by fraud" - he asked where it was from - I said, "From Cox and Greenwood" - he said, "I know nothing about it - I will go there with you" - I said, "Do you know a person named Wainwright?"- he said, "I do - how much is it for?" - I said, "I really do not remember the sum" - he said, "Oh, it is for 50l.: here, I will give you the money" - I declined to take it, and he offered to go to Cox and Greenwood, to take the money there - he also wished me to take him to the Solicitor's in Harley-street, and he would give the money there - I declined to go there with him, and took him to the office - he complained of his friends neglecting him, and said he was obliged to do it - he had no other means of living.
CAPTAIN HENRY MAXWELL WAINWRIGHT. I was lately a Captain in the 47th. I am acquainted with Captain Gordon, who is in Ireland, and have been so for eighteen years - I never heard of any other Captain Wainwright with whom Captain Gordon was acquainted - I have left the 47th three years - I have been acquainted with the 47th since 1808 - I was in it twenty years - I never knew any other Captain Wainwright, but myself - I do not know of any Captain Charles Wainwright, in the army - Captain Gordon was at my house about half a year ago - I did not authorize any person to sign that order for me - I knew nothing of the transaction till after this discovery.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You knew nothing of this cheque? A. No; my name is not Charles - I was acquainted with the prisoner about three years ago - he knew my Christain name is not Charles, and he knew I had belonged to the 47th - Captain Gordon is in health as far as I know - he is with his regiment at Mullingar - I have notseen him for half a year - the signature to this letter is his handwriting - I do not know what acquaintance he may have made within the last half-year - I have been all over India, and never knew any Captain Wainwright, but myself - there may be one in the militia that I do not know - I knew a Mr. Campbell in the regiment, intimately - he was acquainted with Captain Gordon - he died suddenly - I do not think Campbell knew the prisoner.
MR. BODKIN. Q. During your acquaintance with the prisoner, did you ever know him as Captain Wainwright? A. No.
JURY. Q. Did you expect such an order as that from Captain Gordon? A. None - I do not live at the British Hotel, nor are my letters directed there.
(Letter read.)
"To Captain Wainwright, British Hotel, Cockspur-street.
"Mullingar Barracks, 10th December, 1833.
"Gentlemen, - Please to pay Captain Wainwright 50l., and charge the same to my account; giving directions to your broker to sell out from the 4 per cents a sum equivalent to meet that, by virtue of the power of attorney you hold from me.
"To Messrs. Cox and Co."JOHN GORDON,
"1st Captain 47th Regt."
- POWELL. Our house are agents to Captain Wainwright - we had a power of attorney to sell this stock for Captain Gordon.
Prisoner's Defence. My Lord, and Gentlemen of the Jury, although a gentleman by birth and education, I am so paralyzed by the disgrace and ignominy of my present situation, that I have not power to address you verbally, although my life or liberty might be the forfeit of my silence. I must therefore trust to this less effective mode of expressing to you the truth of my case. My father was a man of high respectability, nearly related to the banker and the baronet of the same name; he was an East India director, and I need not add therefore of good fortune. The wants of a man are more or less in proportion to his station in life. My life was that of a gentleman moving in the best society, and to keep a footing with those I associated with, required pecuniary means of considerable extent, and those means were supplied by the hand of a kind and affectionate parent, while my father lived; unfortunately he died, in 1832, and then, for the first time, it was found that his affairs were insolvent, and I, of course, destitute and pennyless. I was of no profession, for I had quitted the army in my father's lifetime, and of course had great difficulty to live and support myself and wife. I had some trifling assistance from friends, but that of course could not last, and I was unable to extricate myself from my embarrassments, and was obliged to keep myself secret and change my name; and, for fear of being arrested, took the name of Wainwright, and went to Ireland, where a friend of mine was with his regiment; this friend was Captain Duncan Campbell, of the 47th: he knew me both by my own name of Prescott, and by the assumed name of Wainwright; and, as I had held a commission in a dragoon regiment, I had (although retired from the service) been generally since styled Captain, which accounts for my being called Captain Wainwright, as I had been called Captain Prescott; and so friendly was Captain Campbell with me, that he advised me to continue the same name, and I corresponded with him by that name when I left Ireland and came to London. At length, my distress and difficulty increasing, I wrote to him for the loan of 50l., and an order came for the amount from a Captain Gordon; this order was addressed to me as "Captain Wainwright, British Hotel, Cockspur-street, London." I there received it, as I had received other letters, and then, as has been stated, presented it for payment on the one day, and received the amount on a subsequent day. Now, gentlemen, where is the forgery? Was I not the person who wrote to Captain Campbell? If I were not the person intended to have the money, who was? My brother-in-law's name, 'tis true, is Wainwright; his name is not however Charles Wainwright, but Henry Maxwell Wainwright; he lives at Aske, near Farnham, in Surrey, forty miles from London. Did he write to Captain Campbell or Captain Gordon for the loan of money? I humbly submit that it is the duty of the prosecutors, before they ask for a verdict of guilty, to convince you that I am not the person who was intended to have this money, by producing the person who was. Captain Gordon intended it for the person who wrote to Captain Campbell; and I am that person, otherwise I should not have received the letter addressed to me. Again, when I presented the order the amount was not paid, but I was directed to return some days after. Would a guilty man have returned? No; he would have sent a messenger, or done any act rather than ventured again to the same spot. I had no such fear; I was doing nothing wrong; and I returned and signed the draft which the clerk placed before me. If any other person had been in expectation of such order for 50l., that person would have gone to the British Hotel, for the letter, and to Cox and Greenwood's to inquire. Is there any proof that any, even the least, inquiry was made there for the letter? and if any other person but myself had expected it, and had been disappointed, would not that person have been a witness here to-day, and complained that he had been defrauded of the 50l. which had been sent him? Gentlemen, the facts resolve themselves into this - I was distressed, and in fear of my creditors, and to avoid arrest, assumed the name of Captain Charles Wainwright: my friend, Captain Campbell, knew it, and corresponded with me accordingly. I wrote to him for a loan, which was sent me by his friend; and I signed the name I had assumed, and by which I was known in the transaction. What forgery is this? What was my conduct when apprehended? - The officer said he had a warrant against me for fraud: I said I knew nothing of it. It was true; I knew of no fraud. He said it was from Cox and Greenwood's. Knowing I had committed no fraud, I offered to go there with him, but he refused to allow me: and then he mentioned the name of Wainwright; on which I instantly named the transaction, and the sum received. Did this look like guilt, gentlemen? No; I had nothing to conceal. I had received the money intended for me, and supposed, because I had not repaid it, that I was to be arrested for the amount, and, of course, was anxious to avoid a prison, not merely on account of the 50l., but for fear of other creditors. I certainly complained of the neglect of my friends, and my want of means to live,and said, I was obliged to do as I had done, referring thereby to the loan I had made of the 50l. These are the facts, my Lord and Gentlemen, and I trust you will see that in writing the name by which I was known to the person to whom I wrote for the money, I did nothing dishonest, or fraudulent; and with full assurance on your judgment, I rely on your verdict of acquittal.
JURY to WILLIAM BALLARD. Q. Was it possible for the prisoner to mistake you for a sheriff's officer? A. He knew me as an officer of Marlbourough-street - I have been so ten or twelve years - I never knew him go by the name of Wainwright.
JAMES POWELL. No application has been made for this money in the name of Wainwright; nor has anybody claimed it - I should not have paid it to anybody but a person named Wainwright.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340410-206]
729. RICHARD LENNOX was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2s. 6d., the goods of James Engleburt, from his person.
JAMES ENGLEBURT. On the 3rd of April, about half-past nine o'clock, I was opposite Shoreditch church - I felt a snatch at my pocket, turned round, and saw the prisoner walking before me - I ran before him and saw a handkerchief in his hand - he immediately passed it to another person, and I have not seen it since - I secured him immediately - I am certain he had it in his possession - I could not swear it was mine.
JAMES CHARLES WOLLER. I was with the prosecutor who missed his handkerchief - he charged the prisoner with it - he appeared confused - I did not see it in his hand.
Prisoner's Defence. I knocked up against the gentleman, and he charged me with stealing his handkerchief - he searched me, but I had not got it - he let me go, then came after me again, and gave me in charge.
JAMES ENGLEBURT re-examined. Nobody else could have picked my pocket - no body else was near him - he was about three feet from me - my handkerchief was red, yellow, and black - I could discover the handkerchief he had was those colours, and fully believe it to be mine - I felt my pocket picked the moment before, and nobody was so near me as him - I saw it go out of his hand immediately - I can swear that no person was near me except the prisoner when I felt my pocket picked.
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Recommended to mercy. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18340410-207]
730. ELIZA BUTLER was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of March, 18 yards of ribbon, value 11s., the goods of Enoch Jones.
WILLIAM HOLLIDAY. I am shopman to Enoch Jones, a draper, in the City-road. The prisoner came to the shop on the 4th of March - she bought a yard of ribbon and paid for it - I saw her take this ribbon, and put it under her shawl - I took her to Mr. Jones - she dropped it when she was accused of stealing it.
ENOCH JONES. I am the master of the shop - this is my ribbon - I heard something fall, and found it at her feet.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18340410-208]
731. JOHN BARKER was indicted for a misdemeanor.
WILLIAM OLDHAM. I am shopman to William Sowter, a builder. I had known the prisoner about a month in the employ of Mr. Jay, the foreman at Sowter's - I considered he was in the employ of Jay as usual - he came to our yard, and said he had come from Mr. Jay for some nails to go to Aldersgate-street - knowing Mr. Sowter was building there, I gave him between two and three thousand nails to take there, believing Jay had sent him for them.
NELSON CHALCROFT. On the 8th of March, I met the prisoner coming out of the yard with some nails - he said he had got a bit of a job of his own, and had come after a few nails, and not to say any thing about it.
JOHN JAY. I am foreman to Mr. Sowter. I did not send the prisoner for any nails on the 8th of March - I have known him two or three years - he bore a very good character.
GUILTY. Aged 34. - Recommended to mercy. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18340410-209]
NEW COURT, Wednesday, April 16, 1834.
Fourth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
732. ABRAHAM GREEN and ELIZABETH GREEN were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 2 violins, value 15s., the goods of George Adam Barnes.
GEORGE ADAM BARNES. I live in the Shepherdess-walk, City-road, and deal in furniture. On Monday, the 17th of March, I missed two violins - I had seen them safe about six o'clock on the Saturday evening - I went to a pawnbroker's to make inquiries, and saw Elizabeth Green, the prisoner, there offering one of my violins to pawn - the pawnbroker said it had been pawned on the Saturday night by a tall young man, and had been taken out again - I asked how she got it - she said her husband bought it of a tall young man - she described him as the pawnbroker had done - I sent for an officer, who took her - she sent for her husband, who gave the same account - he was then taken before the magistrate, and let out on bail - he called on me in about a week, and said a friend of his had picked up part of a duplicate of a violin - we went to a pawnbroker's in St. Luke's, and found this other violin, which is mine.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340410-210]
733. JOHN EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 1 pair of boots, value 5s., the goods of Josiah Storer and another.
ERNEST STORER. I live with my brother, Josiah Storer and another in Whitechapel - they are boot and shoemakers. On the 7th of April, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, I was standing at their door, and these boots were presented to me - they are my brother's - I took them - they had hung inside our door.
JAMES WILD (police-constable R 141). I was in Whitechapel in plain clothes - I saw the prisoner and two others at the prosecutor's window - I went to the corner of Petticoat-lane - I watched them a minute or two - they went to the shop window and then to the foot path, and then to the window again - I went to the foot path, and saw the prisoner put his hand on the rail that goes round the shop, and take off this pair of boots - he came past me - Icaught at him - he dropped the boots - I took them up - he stooped - I missed my hold of him - he ran up the butchers' row, and there he threw off his own shoes, and ran into Somerset-street - I pursued and took him without losing sight of him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340410-211]
734. WILLIAM CLARK was indicted for stealing, on April 7, 1 silver spoon, value 20s., the goods of Peter Rainer.
CHARLES WALTER. I am valet to Peter Rainer, Esq., of Albany, Piccadilly. The prisoner was his groom, and occasionally assisted me to clean the plate - this spoon is my master's, but I did not miss it till the officer came to inquire about it: and on comparing it with those of the same pattern, I have no doubt of its being my master's, though the crest is partly taken off.
NATHANIEL BOARD. I am foreman to a pawnbroker in Green-street, Leicester-square. This spoon was offered in pawn by James Jennery, on the 7th of April - I thought it was not right, and asked who he brought it from - he said from Mrs. Henderson - I sent him back to send the person to me - he did not return - I had occasion to go to Marlborough-street, and showed the spoon to Avis, the officer - on the following day the prisoner came to me - he said he had come for a spoon which had been left by an old man yesterday - I said he could not expect me to give it up to him, as he was not the person who left it - he said he would go and fetch the man who left it - he did so - I said I should not give it them - I sent for the officer, but he was not in the way - the prisoner went away, and came again, and said, when he came the second time, that he was surprised at my keeping the spoon, and he was rather impudent - I gave him in charge.
JAMES JENNERY. I am a coachman. I have known the prisoner some time - I was going to get half a pint of porter - he came to me and said, "I wish you would go on an errand for me" - I said, "What was it to do?" - he said"To pawn a spoon" - I said "I am not in the habit of doing such things" - he said he wanted 8s. for it - I took it to the pawnbroker's, and it was detained - I went and told him, and he said he would go to Mrs. Sanders, somewhere by Soho-square - I saw him the next day - he asked me if I would go down to the pawnbroker's - I went with him - he demanded it, and said he would swear it was his property.
GEORGE AVIS. I am an officer. I had charge of the prisoner - this is the spoon - the prisoner said he would prove it was his own property, and that it and another were given to him by a Mrs. Howard.
Prisoner's Defence. I had Mrs. Sanders here, who would prove that she gave me the duplicate of the two spoons which had belonged to Mrs. Howard, with whom I lived before her husband died.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18340410-212]
735. WILLIAM BENSON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 91 yards of cloth, value 80l.; 8 waistcoats, value 5l.; 19 waistcoat-pieces, value 5l.; 4 coats, value 15l.; 11 yards of kerseymere, value 5l.; 4 yards of serge, value 12s.; 2 yards of velvet, value 36s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 30s.; and 2 fur collars, value 10s.; the goods of William Broughton, his master.
WILLIAM BROUGHTON. I am a tailor, and live in High Holborn. The prisoner was in my employ up to the 19th of March - I went out that evening from ten minutes to a quarter past nine o'clock with Thomas Leekey - I left the prisoner in the shop - I gave him directions to clear the window, and shut up the shop - he asked me if I was going to Mr. Gibson 's, the Craven Arms - I said yes, I believed I should, and he said he should come as soon as he had closed - he did not come there, but about a quarter before ten o'clock, a man in the employ of Mr. Thwaites, came for me - I came home, and found my shop stripped of every thing that was moveable, almost: and two or three policemen were in the shop - when I went out there were ninety-one yards of cloth there, worth about 82l.; eight waistcoats, worth 5l.; nineteen waistcoat-pieces, worth 5l.; four coats, worth 15l.; and the other articles stated in the indictment - I have not seen any of them since - the prisoner was gone when I got home - there were no marks of violence on the door, as if any one had broken in - the prisoner did not sleep in my house - he generally came in the morning, but the police took him the next morning.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. These things would have formed a considerable bulk? A. Yes. I went out that evening with Mr. Leekey, who lives in the same house - we both took the house together - he is also a tailor - he is not in partnership with me - he has a weekly salary - I know these bills, which are headed "Leekey and Broughton"- we had been in partnership, but that had been dissolved six weeks before this robbery - on the 16th of January - none of these bills have been given out since, to my knowledge - Leekey was before the magistrate - I believe he stated that he was not a partner with me, but I was in such a state, I could attend to nobody's evidence but my own - sometimes the lock of my door does not catch if the window has not been shut right - I believe the door was found open one morning by a person who was in my employ - I have not been forced to open it with a poker - it is possible that if the window had not been shut right, a person might push the door open - there was nothing of mine found on the prisoner, or about him.
COURT. Q. Was it his business to leave the window and door secure? A. Yes; if he had shut the window, and placed the post right, the door would shut itself when he went out.
ANN BROUGHTON. I am the prosecutor's wife - I went into the shop that evening, about a quarter before ten o'clock- I found every thing had been taken out of it - Bray had come up stairs about a quarter or twenty minutes before ten o'clock, and I asked him how he got in; and from what he told me, I went down to the shop - I found the shop-door ajar, but there was no appearance of its having been forced open - the tickets of prices which had been on the clothes, were strewed about the bottom of the window - they ought to have been in a drawer, or on the mantelpiece - I had never seen them so strewed about before - I had not been in the shop that evening from a quarter past nine o'clock, till a quarter before ten o'clock, but I had passed down the stair-case three times - there is a staircase door which leads into the shop, and each time I came down it was shut - it is usually kept open in the evening.
JOSEPH BRAY. I am twelve years old - I know thenecessity of telling the truth - I lodge at Mr. Broughton's; but I am errand-boy at No. 29, St. Martin's-lane - I got home to Mr. Broughton's on the night in question, about a quarter before ten o'clock - as I was crossing the road opposite his shop, I saw a man outside pushing the door open - he looked round and saw me, and pulled the door to again - he then walked away towards Middle-row - I went in at the door which he had left ajar, and went up stairs - I met Mrs. Broughton on the stairs, and she asked me how I got in - I saw the shop-windows were empty, and the blocks were naked - the man who came out at the shop door was a tall man - I knew the prisoner before - I do not know whether he was the man who came out of the door - I did not see his face - he walked along, and another man, not so tall as he was, joined him.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you lived in that house? A. I do not know, Sir - I believe about a month - I had seen the prisoner constantly going in and out - I leave my place in St. Martin's-lane at ten o'clock at night - my master had not given me a holiday, and sent me home earlier that night than usual - I do not know what time it was when I got to Mr. Broughton's; but my usual time of leaving St. Martin's-lane is ten o'clock - one of the men was a tall man - I do not know whether he was the prisoner - when Mrs. Broughton came into the shop, the door was shut - I shut it when I went in.
COURT. Q. Was the man who came out and shut the door taller or shorter, or about the same height as the prisoner? A. I think about the same height.
ELIZABETH LEEKEY. I am the wife of Thomas Leekey. I lodge at the prosecutor's.
